{}_{2}F_{1}\,\left(1,\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi},2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi};w\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}\frac{e^{-t_{1}}e^{-t_{2}}}{1-e^{-t_{1}\frac{2\pi}{\alpha^{\prime}}}e^{-t_{2}\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\alpha^{\prime}}}w}

(115)

Proof.

By the definition of hypergeometric functions, and manipulations of the various Pochhammer symbols, one can show that
{}_{2}F_{1}\,\left(1,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi},2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi};w\right)
is defined by the series

\begin{split}&{}_{2}F_{1}\,\left(1,\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi},2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi};w\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{n}}{(1+\frac{2\pi n}{\alpha^{\prime}})(1+\frac{2\pi n}{2\pi+\alpha^{\prime}})}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}e^{-t_{1}(1+\frac{2\pi n}{\alpha^{\prime}})}e^{-t_{2}(1+\frac{2\pi n}{2\pi+\alpha^{\prime}})}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}\frac{e^{-t_{1}}e^{-t_{2}}}{1-e^{-t_{1}\frac{2\pi}{\alpha^{\prime}}}e^{-t_{2}\frac{2\pi}{2\pi+\alpha^{\prime}}}w}\end{split}

(116)

∎

Combining Eqs. (
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
114
), we get

-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n}(2\sin(\alpha/2))^{2}\mathcal{J}_{2n}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\left\{\int_{0}^{\alpha}d\alpha^{\prime}-\int_{-\alpha}^{0}d\alpha^{\prime}\right\}\sin\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}d\tilde{w}_{2n}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\tilde{w}_{1}\,\frac{\left(\frac{\tilde{w}_{1}}{\tilde{w}_{2n}}\right)^{-\alpha^{\prime}/2\pi}}{(\tilde{w}_{2n}+\tilde{w}_{1})^{2}}\Big(1-e^{-D(\tilde{w}_{2n}+\tilde{w}_{1})}\Big)+O(D^{-1/3})

(117)

Now, we can perform the first two integrals over
\tilde{w}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}_{2n}
in Mathematica and arrive at

\begin{split}&-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n}(2\sin(\alpha/2))^{2}\mathcal{J}_{2n}+O(D^{-1/3})\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\left\{\int_{0}^{\alpha}d\alpha^{\prime}-\int_{-\alpha}^{0}d\alpha^{\prime}\right\}\Bigg\{\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime}D^{1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}}\frac{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}}\cdot{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,-D\right]\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+2\cdot D^{1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}}\,\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\right)\left(e^{-D}-\left(D-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\,{e^{-Dt}}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}}\right)\Bigg\}\end{split}

(118)

In the above,
\Gamma
is the Gamma function and

{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,-D\right]

is the generalized hypergeometric function
Bailey 1935
.
Note that the second line above can be uniformly bounded so only contributes at most an
O(1/D)
factor. We have the following lemma to analyze the other term.

Lemma 15
.

The hypergeometric function

{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,w\right]

is an analytic continuation of the following integral.

{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,w\right]=\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}e^{-t_{1}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}e^{-t_{2}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\exp\left[w\,e^{-t_{1}}e^{-t_{2}}\right].

(119)

Proof.

By the definition of hypergeometric functions, and manipulations of the various Pochhammer symbols, one can show that
{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,w\right]

is defined by the series

\begin{split}&{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,w\right]=\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{n}}{n!}\frac{1}{\left(n+1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}}\\
&=\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{n}}{n!}e^{-t_{1}\left(n+1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}e^{-t_{2}\left(n+1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\\
&=\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}e^{-t_{1}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}e^{-t_{2}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\exp\left[w\,e^{-t_{1}}e^{-t_{2}}\right].\end{split}

(120)

∎

This gives

\begin{split}&D^{1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}}\frac{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)^{2}}\cdot{}_{2}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\\
2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\,\,,&\,\,2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\end{matrix}\,\,;\,\,-D\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{2}e^{-(t_{1}+t_{2}-\log(D))\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\exp\left[-\,e^{-(t_{1}+t_{2}-\log(D))}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\int_{-\log(D)}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\log(D))e^{-t^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\exp\left[-e^{-t^{\prime}}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\log(D))e^{-t^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\exp\left[-e^{-t^{\prime}}\right]+O(1/D)\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}d{t^{\prime\prime}}(-\log(t^{\prime\prime})+\log(D)){t^{\prime\prime}}^{\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)-1}e^{-t^{\prime\prime}}+O(1/D)=\log(D)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}+O(1/D).\end{split}

(121)

The first equality above uses Lemma
15
and simplification. The second equality follows from setting
t^{\prime}=t_{1}+t_{2}-\log(D)
and doing the integral over
t_{1}-t_{2}
. In the third equality, the integral over
t^{\prime}:-\infty\to-\log(D)
is an
O(1/D)
term since the integrand is super-exponentially suppressed so we can add it in. In the fourth equality, we substitute
t^{\prime\prime}=e^{-t^{\prime}}
. In the last equality,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the derivative of the Gamma function and note that the integration of the term corresponding to
\log(t^{\prime\prime})
gives
\Gamma^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)
and the term corresponding to
\log(D)
gives
\log(D)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)
.

Finally, using Eqs. (
118
,
121
) we get

\begin{split}&-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n}(2\sin(\alpha/2))^{2}\mathcal{J}_{2n}+O(D^{-1/3})=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\left\{\int_{0}^{\alpha}d\alpha^{\prime}-\int_{-\alpha}^{0}d\alpha^{\prime}\right\}\alpha^{\prime}\left(1+\log(D)-\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2\pi}\right)}\right)\\
&=-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(1+\log(D)+\gamma_{\mathrm{Euler}}\right)-4\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\right)^{2n}\zeta(2n-1)\end{split}

(122)

where
\zeta
is the Riemann-zeta function.

Appendix D
2D Free Fermion Determinants and Tau Functions

We warn the reader that throughout this section the computations should be taken to be formal, and the results of this section aren’t meant to be mathematically rigorous. However, these formal manipulations match the rigorous calculations of this paper for the dimer model, of Sec.
IV
.

The tau function as introduced by
Jimbo et al. 1981
, is a function that’s holomorphic in the variables
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
, depending on a flat connection
\rho
on the punctured plane
\mathbb{C}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
. A more closely related viewpoint that we take is related to that of Palmer
Palmer 1993
who interprets the tau function as some regularized determinant of a Cauchy-Riemann operator in the presence of a singular gauge field that’s flat away from the punctures
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
.

The perspective we take in this paper is to formally consider the ‘determinant’ of the two-dimensional Dirac operator
\not{\partial}(p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}|\rho)
in the presence of such a flat connection
\rho
with respect to punctures
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
. Normalizing with the determinant of the starnard Dirac operator
\not{\partial}
we will get a function

\tau(p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}|\rho):=\frac{\det\Big(\not{\partial}(p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}|\rho)\Big)}{\det\big(\not{\partial}\big)}

(123)

This function will differ from the original tau function of
Jimbo et al. 1981
in two ways, which is why we refer to our functions as ‘modified tau functions’.

The first difference is that
\log\tau(p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}|\rho)
will be defined in terms of a series expansion of integrals, many of which are actually divergent integrals. Even though this series expansion may seem ill-defined, in our paper in the context of dimer models, the integrals in the series expansion are naturally regulated by the lattice scale and diverge logarithmically with this scale.
The second is that our series expansion of
\log\tau
is actually not holomorphic in the
p_{i}
, but rather is a sum of a holomorphic and antiholomorphic functions. The holomorphic part can be identified with the original Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno tau function while the antiholomorphic part can be identified with a similar ‘conjugate’ version (see the end of Sec.
D.4
).

In Sec.
D.1
, we will review the gauge-theoretic setup and the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno definition of the tau function in their original vocabulary in terms of Fuschian systems, and then compare the Fuschian representations to the zipper representations of flat gauge fields. In Sec.
D.2
, we review the calculus of computing determinants of Dirac operators and show how it’s gauge-invariant. Then in Sec.
D.3
, we will show how the Fuschian representation of the gauge field gives a representation of
\log\tau
that satisfies the defining Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations. In Sec.
D.4
, we show how the alternate series expansion for
\tau
encountered in dimer correlations (see Sec.
IV
) can be derived using the zipper representation of the gauge fields.

D.1
Singular Gauge Fields and Isomonodromic Tau functions

Consider the punctured plane
\mathbb{C}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
and a connection
\rho
that’s flat away from the punctures. Furthermore, suppose that the monodromy of
\rho
around infinity is zero, so that
\rho
can be viewed as a flat connection on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
.
In our setup, we will consider
GL(N,\mathbb{C})
-connection, which can be viewed as an
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-valued 1-form
A
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. We will mostly consider connections that satisfy the
flatness condition

dA-A\wedge A=0
away from the punctures. In local (real) coordinates
x,y
on
\mathbb{C}
or equivalently the (complex) coordinates
z=x+iy
and
\overline{z}=x-iy
, we will have

\begin{split}&A=A_{x}(x,y)dx+A_{y}(x,y)dy=A_{z}(z,\overline{z})dz+A_{\overline{z}}(z,\overline{z})d\overline{z}\\
&A_{x}=A_{z}+A_{\overline{z}}\quad,\quad A_{y}=iA_{z}-iA_{\overline{z}}\\
&\partial_{y}A_{x}-\partial_{x}A_{y}-[A_{x},A_{y}]=0\quad\mathrm{iff}\quad\partial_{\overline{z}}A_{z}-\partial_{z}A_{\overline{z}}-[A_{z},A_{\overline{z}}]=0,\end{split}

(124)

where the last equation holds when we consider flat gauge fields.
We say that a (local)
N
-component field
\Psi
is a
(local) flat section
of
A
if it satisfies the equation
(d-A)\Psi=0
, which gives a pair of differential equations that
\Psi
needs to satisfy.

Note for any
GL(N,\mathbb{C})
-valued
gauge transformation

g(x,y)
, we have

(d-A)\Psi=0\quad\mathrm{iff}\quad(d-(gAg^{-1}-gdg^{-1}))(g\Psi)

(125)

As such, the flatness of
\Psi
with respect to
A
is equivalent to the flatness of flatness of
g\Psi
with respect to
gAg^{-1}-gdg^{-1}
. As such, we say that two gauge fields
A,A^{\prime}
are
gauge equivalent
if there exists a function
g
such that
A^{\prime}=gAg^{-1}-gdg^{-1}
.

Via the path-ordered exponential, a
basis
local flat sections
\{\Psi_{1},\cdots,\Psi_{N}\}
of any flat gauge field on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
exist on any simply connected subset of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
. However, solutions will often not be consistent in a loop going around a puncture, and will instead have a
monodromy
going around a loop associated to crossings with branch cuts coming out from the punctures. For example, the solution
\Psi=\left(\frac{z}{z-1}\right)^{\alpha}
is a holomorphic solution to
\left(\partial_{z}-\alpha\left(\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z-1}\right)\right)\Psi=0
,
\partial_{\overline{z}}\Psi=0
, but for
\alpha\not\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\Psi
has a branch cut along paths connecting
0\to 1
. In general, given a basepoint
p
in
\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
, the path-ordered exponential associates flat connections to
monodromy representations

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\})\to GL(N,\mathbb{C})
. Under gauge transformation
g
, such representations are equivalent up to conjugation by
g(p)\in GL(N,\mathbb{C})
. And, any two flat connections with the same monodromy representation are gauge equivalent.

Another way to think about the monodromy representation is as follows.
We can fix a simply connected subset by choosing a collection of
k
non-intersecting segments, each of which start at one of the
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
and end at some common point
p
, where the clockwise order of the zippers coming out from
p
are
p_{1}\to p_{2}\to\cdots\to p_{s}
.
We often refer to such segments as ‘zippers’ in analogy with the lattice case.
Cutting
\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
along a small neighborhood of these zippers will leave a simply connected region.
And, we can construct a matrix of solutions
{\bf\Psi}=(\Psi_{1},\cdots,\Psi_{N})
with
(d-A){\bf\Psi}=0
in this region and smooth it out near the zippers.
Choosing a gauge transformation
g={\bf\Psi}^{-1}
will mean that in the region away from the cuts, we’ll have that the transformed gauge field is zero, since
A^{\prime}=-{\bf\Psi}^{-1}(d-A){\bf\Psi}=0
.
After making this gauge transformation, the path-ordered exponential going from left-to-right across the zippers coming from
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
will be associated to monodromy matrices
M_{1},\cdots,M_{s}
, which generate the flat connection
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\})\to GL(N,\mathbb{C})
. Sometimes, we refer to the
\{M_{j}\}
as
jump matrices
. Because there’s no monodromy around the point
p
where the zippers meet, there’s an additional ‘flatness condition’ that
M_{s}\cdots M_{1}=\text{1}
. In general, the zippers need not meet at a single point, and any graph with endpoints at
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
will work. If
M_{1},\cdots,M_{s}
are associated to loops going around
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
, the matrices going along the edges of any such graph are subject to a flatness condition around each vertex of the graph that’s not
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
. See Fig.
23
for a depiction. We use these zipper representations in Sec.
D.4
.

Figure 23:
A gauge transformation can transform a flat gauge into one that’s zero in a simply connected region away from some set of ‘zippers’ which have matrices attached to them according to what the path-ordered exponential assigns going across the zipper. Around points that aren’t
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
where the zippers meet, there is a flatness condition for the matrices.

A related problem of finding holomorphic solutions to so-called
Fuschian systems
can be phrased this way.
For some points
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\in\mathbb{C}
and
n\times n
matrices
B_{1},\cdots,B_{s}
satisfying
\sum_{j}B_{j}=0
, consider the differential equations

\partial_{z}\Psi=\left(\sum_{j}\frac{B_{j}}{z-p_{j}}\right)\Psi\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\partial_{\overline{z}}\Psi=0.

(126)

Local solutions to these equations are equivalent to finding local flat sections of a connection with
A_{z}=\sum_{j}\frac{B_{j}}{z-p_{j}}
,
A_{\overline{z}}=0
. Note that away from the
p_{j}
,
dA-A\wedge A=0
so
A
flat. The condition
\sum_{j}B_{j}=0
means that there’s no pole of
A_{z}
at infinity, so the gauge field is non-singular if extended to
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.

Since the Fuschian connection is flat, any points
p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}
and matrices
B_{1},\cdots,B_{s}
are associated to monodromy matrices
M_{1},\cdots,M_{s}
up to overall conjugation, as in the zipper construction above.

10
10

10

In fact, the inverse problem of constructing matrices construct matrices
B_{1},\cdots,B_{s}
given points
\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s}\}
given monodromy data
\{M_{1},\cdots,M_{s}\}
, known as Hilbert’s 21st problem, can be solved for
generic
monodromy matrices
\{M_{1},\cdots,M_{s}\}
(c.f.
Bolibrukh 1995
).

In general, small deformations the
\{p_{j}\}
or of the matrices
\{B_{j}\}
will lead to deformations in the monodromy matrices
\{M_{j}\}
. The problem of finding
isomonodromic deformations
asks: given a small deformation of the
\{p_{j}\}
, what deformations of
\{B_{j}\}
are necessary to keep the monodromy data fixed? The following
Schlesinger Equations

\begin{split}\partial_{p_{k}}B_{i}=\frac{[B_{k},B_{i}]}{p_{k}-p_{i}},\,\,\text{ if }k\neq i\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\partial_{p_{k}}B_{k}=-\sum_{\ell\neq k}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\end{split}

(127)

give sufficient equations for finding isomonodromic deformations. Note that the second equation above follows from the condition that
\sum_{j}B_{j}=0
. We will find it convenient to write these more compactly as

\partial_{p_{k}}B_{i}=\frac{[B_{k},B_{i}]}{p_{k}-p_{i}}-\delta_{k,i}\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}

(128)

with the understanding that the ‘singular’ terms “
\frac{[B_{j},B_{j}]}{p_{j}-p_{j}}
” are set to zero.

In
Jimbo et al. 1981
Jimbo, Miwa, Ueno constructed the differential form
\omega

\begin{split}\omega:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{i}B_{j})}{p_{i}-p_{j}}d(p_{i}-p_{j}),\end{split}

(129)

on the parameter space of points
\{p_{j}\}
. From Eq. (
127
), one finds
d\omega=0
, so that there are
local
solutions
d\log\tau=\omega
, or equivalently of the equations

\partial_{p_{i}}\log\tau=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{i}B_{j})}{p_{i}-p_{j}}

(130)

This local function
\tau
(defined up to multiplication by an overall scalar or antiholomorphic function) is the isomodromic tau function of Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno.

D.2
Determinants of 2D Dirac operators, Counterterms, and Gauge Invariance

Here we will review general series expansions for determinants of 2D Dirac operators, also known as partition functions of 2D free Dirac fermions, in the presence of a background gauge fields. The lesson is that the naive series expansion for Dirac determinants in a background gauge field is not actually gauge invariant, but one can add a local counterterm making a gauge-invariant partition function. Our presentation is motivated by notes of Nair
Nair 2005
, for whom perspective is equating this fermion determinant with the action of the WZW theory
Witten 1984
(see also
Polyakov and Wiegmann 1983
). Our discussion sidesteps WZW theory and derives things more directly.

Let
A
be any
\mathfrak{gl}(N,\mathbb{C})
gauge field with components
A_{z},A_{\overline{z}}
. Throughout this section, we’ll assume that
A
decays quickly, at least quadratically approaching infinity. In two dimensions, the 2D Dirac operator in the presence of this gauge field is the operator

\not{\partial}-\not{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{z}\\
\partial_{\overline{z}}&0\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}0&A_{z}\\
A_{\overline{z}}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{z}-A_{z}\\
\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}&0\end{pmatrix}

(131)

formed from the operators
\not{\partial}
and
\not{A}
.
acting on ‘spinors’, which for our purposes are pairs of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
-valued functions
\psi_{L},\psi_{R}
arranged into
\mathbb{C}^{2N}
-valued ones as
\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{L}\\
\psi_{R}\end{pmatrix}
. The components
\psi_{L}
and
\psi_{R}
are the ‘left’ and ‘right’ components of the spinor.

Our goal is to make sense of the expression
\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)
. The fact that
\not{\partial}-\not{A}
is an infinite-dimensional linear operator indicates that such expressions are likely singular and ill-defined, even in a physical sense. We’ll have more luck if instead we consider the ratio of the above determinant with
\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)
. Formally, we can expand the above quantity’s logarithm as

\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\log\det\left[1-\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right]=\mathrm{tr}\log\left[1-\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right]=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right)^{n}\right],

(132)

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}
refers to an equality in the sense of formal series.
In the above,
\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}
refers to the Green’s function

\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}(x_{1},x_{2})\\
\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}(x_{1},x_{2})&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}}&0\end{pmatrix}

(133)

which is formed by the individual Green’s functions
\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}}
and
\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}
.

In the above, for each coordinate
x_{j}=(x_{j,1},x_{j,2})
, we denote
z_{j}=x_{j,1}+ix_{j,2}
and
\overline{z}_{j}=x_{j,1}-ix_{j,2}
. Throughout this section, we will use this notation, where ‘
x
’ refers to the coordinate in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
z
amd
\overline{z}
refer to the corresponding complex coordinate in
\mathbb{C}
and its conjugate.

Now, we should make sense of the ‘matrix multiplication’ and traces above. The space we’re acting on is
\mathbb{C}^{2N}
tensored with some suitable function space (e.g.
L^{2}(\mathbb{C}),C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
). As such, linear operators on this space live in
M_{2N\times 2N}(\mathbb{C})
tensored with linear operators on the function space. Given two operators
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}
, we’ll have
(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2})(x_{1},x_{2})=\int dz^{\prime}\mathcal{O}_{1}(x_{1},x^{\prime})\mathcal{O}_{2}(x^{\prime},x_{2})
, and the quantity
\mathcal{O}_{1}(x_{1},x^{\prime})\mathcal{O}_{2}(x^{\prime},x_{2})
refers to matrix multiplication in
M_{2N\times 2N}(\mathbb{C})
. In this sense, since
\not{A}
is a diagonal matrix, we would express

\not{A}(x_{1},x_{2})=\not{A}(x_{1})\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})

(134)

where
\delta^{2}(z_{1},z_{2})
is a Dirac delta function. This means that

\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}(x_{1},x_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(z_{2})}{z_{1}-z_{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{A_{z}(z_{2})}{\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

(135)

Since this is block-diagonal with respect to the left and right spinor components, we can write

\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}&\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right)^{n}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}A_{\overline{z}}\right)^{n}\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}A_{z}\right)^{n}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n-1}-z_{n})(z_{n}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\cdots A_{z}(x_{n})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n-1}-\overline{z}_{n})(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}\end{split}

(136)

In the above, we integrate over all
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The first term in the brackets comes from the ‘left’ half of the spinor field and the second term comes from the ‘right’ half.

A degenerate case is
n=1
where formally, the expression is

\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}A_{\overline{z}}\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}A_{z}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x_{1}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})\right]}{z_{1}-z_{1}}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})\right]]}{\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{1}}\right\}.

(137)

A priori this seems ill-defined. However, a ‘point-splitting regularization’ that smooths out the Green’s functions
\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}
,
\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}
by bump functions
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x,x^{\prime})
of shrinking width
\epsilon
will create ‘regulated’ Green’s functions. We will choose symmetric, normalized bump functions. For example, a Gaussian kernel
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\pi\epsilon}e^{-|x-x^{\prime}|^{2}/\epsilon}
serves our purpose. This gives a regulated Green’s function

\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}\right)_{\textbf{reg}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int d^{2}x^{\prime}\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}(x_{1},x^{\prime})\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime},x_{2})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}},&\,\,\text{ if }z_{1}\neq z_{2}\\
0,&\,\,\text{ if }z_{1}=z_{2}\\
\end{cases}

(138)

Similarly,
\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}\right)_{\textbf{reg}}
agrees with the unregulated version away from the diagonal and returns zero on the diagonal. This gives that the
n=1
term vanishes.

\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}\int d^{2}x\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x)\right]\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}\right)_{\textbf{reg}}(x,x)+\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x)\right]\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{z}}\right)_{\textbf{reg}}(x,x)\right\}=0,

(139)

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}
refers to this quantity vanishing in this regulated scheme.
In total, this will give a series expansion

\begin{split}\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{form}}}{{=}}\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}+\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{z}-A_{z}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{z}\right)}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\cdots A_{z}(x_{n})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}\end{split}

(140)

Before, we said that the above series expansion is in fact not gauge invariant. First, we can’t expect either of the parts
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{z}-A_{z}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{z}\right)}
and
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}
to be gauge invariant. This is because general gauge fields
A_{z}
and
A_{\overline{z}}
can be written as

A_{z}=-M\partial_{z}M^{-1}\quad,\quad A_{\overline{z}}=-\overline{M}\partial_{\overline{z}}\overline{M}^{-1}

(141)

for some independent matrix-valued fields
M,\overline{M}
.

11
11

11

This follows because for a gauge field
H
, the operators
\partial_{z}-H_{z}
and
\partial_{\overline{z}}-H_{\overline{z}}
are invertible. For example, we have a series

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}-H_{\overline{z}}}(x,x^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}(x,x^{\prime})+\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}H_{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}\right)(x,x^{\prime})+\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}H_{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}H_{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}}\right)(x,x^{\prime})+\cdots\\
&=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{z-z^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[H_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})\right]}{(z-z_{1})(z_{1}-z^{\prime})}+\frac{1}{\pi^{3}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[H_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})H_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]}{(z-z_{1})(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z^{\prime})}+\cdots\end{split}

(142)

which satisfies
\left(\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-H_{\overline{z}}\right)\frac{1}{\partial_{\overline{z}}-H_{\overline{z}}}\right)(x,x^{\prime})=\delta(x,x^{\prime})
.
As such, the flatness condition
\partial_{\overline{z}}\mathcal{A}_{z}-[A_{\overline{z}},\mathcal{A}_{z}]=\partial_{z}A_{\overline{z}}
can be solved for
\mathcal{A}_{z}
, since
\partial_{\overline{z}}-[A_{\overline{z}},\boldsymbol{\cdot}]
is a connection in the adjoint representation. Since the gauge field
(\mathcal{A}_{z},A_{\overline{z}})
is flat on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
,
\overline{M}
can be expressed as the path-ordered exponential with respect to the gauge field. Parallel manipulations work to find the matrix
M
.

So, each individual component
A_{z},A_{\overline{z}}
can be written as a gauge transformation of the trivial field, even though the full field may not. As such, if
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{z}-A_{z}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{z}\right)}
and
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}
were gauge invariant, they’d both be trivial.

At this point, we want to evaluate the variation of each of the summands
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{z}-A_{z}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{z}\right)}
and
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}
with respect to an infinitesimal gauge transformation
e^{\delta u(x)}
, where
\delta u
is small. Under this transformation
A\to e^{\delta u}Ae^{-\delta u}-e^{\delta u}\,d\,e^{-\delta u}
, we have that the gauge field varies as

\delta A_{z}=[\delta u,A_{z}]+\partial_{z}\delta u\quad,\quad\delta A_{\overline{z}}=[\delta u,A_{\overline{z}}]+\partial_{\overline{z}}\delta u

(143)

at first order in
\delta u
. Let’s first examine the variation in the expansion
\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}
. At first order, we’ll have

\begin{split}\delta&\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}-A_{\overline{z}}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left([\delta u(x_{1}),A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})]+\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\delta u(x_{1})\right)A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}\\
&=-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\mathrm{tr}\left[\delta u(x_{1})\left\{\Big[A_{z}(x_{1})\,,\,\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}\Big]\,-\,\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\left(\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}\right)\right\}\right]\\
&=-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\mathrm{tr}\left[\delta u(x_{1})\left\{\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}-\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}\right\}\right]\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\,\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\delta u(x_{1})\,\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\right]\\
&\quad+\sum_{n^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{n^{\prime}}}\pi\int d^{2}x_{2}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n^{\prime}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\delta u(x_{2})\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n^{\prime}})}{(z_{2}-z_{3})\cdots(z_{n^{\prime}}-z_{2})}-\delta u(x_{n^{\prime}})\frac{A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n^{\prime}})}{(z_{n^{\prime}}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{3})\cdots(z_{n^{\prime}-1}-z_{n^{\prime}})}\right]\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\partial_{z}\delta u\right)A_{\overline{z}}\right]\end{split}

(144)

The first equality follows from the product rule and a cyclic reordering of each of the
n
terms, which gets rid of the
\frac{1}{n}
factor. The second equality is two steps. First, we rearrange the commutator using the identity
\mathrm{tr}([\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}])
.
Next, we integrate the other summand by parts, noting that
\int_{\mathcal{R}}d^{2}x\partial_{z}(\cdots)=-2i\int_{\partial\mathcal{R}}d\overline{z}(\cdots)
is a boundary term, which drops away if the integrand decays fast enough.
The third equality does some rearranging uses a partial fractions decomposition together with the equality
\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z^{\prime}}=\pi\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x^{\prime})
before integrating over
x_{1}
. The
n^{\prime}=2
term is a special case and will be handled separately next.
In the fourth equality, notice that in the infinite sums above, pairs of terms with index-labels
n,n^{\prime}
with
n^{\prime}=n+1
cancel each other exactly, so only the
n^{\prime}=2
term remains. Then, we use the fact that
\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}=-\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}{\partial}_{z_{1}}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}=-\pi\partial_{z_{1}}\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})
. Then, the equality follows from integration-by-parts and integrating over
x_{1}
. Similarly, we can express

\delta\log\frac{\det\left(\partial_{z}-A_{z}\right)}{\det\left(\partial_{z}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\delta u\right)A_{z}\right]

(145)

so that the full variation of the regulated series expansion

\delta\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{reg}}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\partial_{z}\delta u\right)A_{\overline{z}}\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\partial_{\overline{z}}\delta u\right)A_{z}\right]\right\}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\delta\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}A_{z}\right].

(146)

As such, the regulated series expansion is
not
gauge invariant which can be said to be a
gauge anomaly
. However, the addition of a
local counterterm

\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}A_{z}\right]
to the series expansion will indeed give us a series for the determinant that’s invariant under infinitesimal gauge transformations.

As such, the series should be the same for ‘small gauge transformations’ that are connected to the identity. However, for ‘large gauge transformations’ that aren’t connected to the identity, we can’t expect that these two determinants are the same. This behavior is also the case for the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno tau function.
For example, the connections
A_{z}=\alpha\left(\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z-1}\right),A_{\overline{z}}=0
and
A^{\prime}_{z}=(\alpha+1)\left(\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z-1}\right),A^{\prime}_{\overline{z}}=0
give the same monodromy representation. But, their corresponding Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations Eq. (
130
) don’t match. Note that the gauge transformation connecting these two connections looks like
g(x)=\frac{z}{1-z}
which is zero or singular or zero at
z=0,1
. So, these large gauge transformations cannot be extended to the punctures of the sphere.

In total, adding this counterterm gives us our final expression for the series expansion

\begin{split}\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}&-\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})\cdots A_{z}(x_{n})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}\\
&+\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}A_{z}\right]\end{split}

(147)

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}
refers to a ‘corrected’ determinant, which we henceforth take to be the definition of the Dirac determinant’s series expansion.

We note that this quadratic term
A_{z}A_{\overline{z}}
mixes the holomorphic and antiholomoprhic sectors, like a mass term would. Indeed, one can find that this term can be derived from doing this whole story with the massive Dirac operator
\not{\partial}+m
and investigating the limit
m\to 0
.

D.2.1
Dirac Determinants of Abelian connections

As an example where we can evaluate the above series, let’s take
A
to be an abelian gauge field, so that
[A_{\overline{z}}(x),A_{\overline{z}}(x^{\prime})]=[A_{z}(x),A_{z}(x^{\prime})]=[A_{\overline{z}}(x),A_{z}(x^{\prime})]=0
for all
x,x^{\prime}
.
Since
A
is abelian, we can symmetrize over the order of the variables in the trace, or equivalently make the replacement

\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}\to\frac{1}{n!}\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\cdots A_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)}\frac{1}{(z_{\sigma(1)}-z_{\sigma(2)})(z_{\sigma(2)}-z_{\sigma(3)})\cdots(z_{\sigma(n)}-z_{\sigma(1)})}

(and similarly for the other term
z\leftrightarrow\overline{z}
) in the expression where
\mathrm{Sym}(n)
is the symmetric group of order
n
.
Then by the next lemma, it turns out that all terms in the series for
n\geq 3
vanish.

Lemma 16
.

Let be
n\geq 3
. Then for
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
distinct,

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)}\frac{1}{(z_{\sigma(1)}-z_{\sigma(2)})(z_{\sigma(2)}-z_{\sigma(3)})\cdots(z_{\sigma(n)}-z_{\sigma(1)})}=0

(148)

A clean proof is done similarly to a similar computation in
Boutillier 2005
.

Proof.

Let
f(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
be the left-hand-side of Eq. (
148
).
Let
V(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(z_{i}-z_{j})
be the Vandermonde determinant.
Note that
f
is manifestly a symmetric function in
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
, whereas
V
is antisymmetric. This means that
f\cdot V
is an antisymmetric function. Moreover, every term in
f\cdot V
is a polynomial in
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
of lower degree than
V
. Since
V
is the lowest degree non-constant antisymmmetric polynomial in
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
, this means that
f\cdot V
must be a constant. For
n>3
, every term has degree greater than zero, so
f\cdot V
must be exactly zero, implying that
f
vanishes. The vanishing for
n=3
can be verified directly.
∎

So for abelian gauge fields, we have the expression

\begin{split}&\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}A_{z}\right]\\
&=+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\Big\{\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]\partial_{z_{1}}\partial_{z_{2}}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})+\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\right]\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\partial_{\overline{z}_{2}}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\right]\partial_{z_{1}}\partial_{\overline{z_{2}}}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})-\mathrm{tr}\left[A_{z}(x_{1})A_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]\partial_{\overline{z_{1}}}\partial_{z_{2}}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})\Big\}\\
&=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\big\{\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})\,\mathrm{tr}[F(x_{1})F(x_{2})]\big\}\end{split}

(149)

where
F=\partial_{y}A_{x}-\partial_{x}A_{y}=-2i(\partial_{\overline{z}}A_{z}-\partial_{z}A_{\overline{z}})
is the field strength of the abelian connection.
In the second equality, we use the facts
\partial_{z}\log(|x-x^{\prime}|^{2})=\frac{1}{z-z^{\prime}}
so
\partial_{\overline{z}^{\prime}}\partial_{z}\log(|x-x^{\prime}|^{2})=\pi\delta^{2}(x-x^{\prime})
so that

\int d^{2}xA_{\overline{z}}A_{z}=\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A_{z}(x_{2})\partial_{\overline{z}_{1}}\partial_{z_{2}}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}).

In the third equality, we do various integrations-by-parts and reorganize terms. For
A
a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
connection, this final result is the same result one would get from coupling the Gaussian Free Field (i.e. free boson) to the field strength
F
, which can be expressed as

\begin{split}&\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\big\{\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})\,F(x_{1})F(x_{2})\big\}=\log\frac{Z_{GFF}(F)}{Z_{GFF}(0)}\\
&\text{where }\quad Z_{GFF}(F)=\int[d\phi]e^{-\int d^{2}x\left\{{\pi}(\partial\phi)^{2}+F(x)\phi(x)\right\}}.\end{split}

(150)

Such a result was known as early as 1962 by Schwinger
Schwinger 1962
and is an example of
bosonization
, where in 2D, certain bosonic and fermion correlators can be equated.

D.3
‘Fuschian’ expansion of
\log\tau
and the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations

Now, we apply our formalism above to compute the determinant with respect to Fuschian connections and show how the expansion satisfies the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations.
Above, we had to manipulate various infinities to justify a gauge-invariant series expansion. Here, we will encounter another set of infinities that come from the singular nature of the connection. To handle these singularities, we will instead consider a ‘smeared out’ determinant where the singular connection is smoothed by a kernel of width
\epsilon
to become a regular connection and consider the expansion around
\epsilon\to 0
.
This will give functions that diverge logarithmically with
\epsilon
.
We conjecture that after appropriately subtracting off the divergence, the limiting series expansion indeed converges to the modified tau function.

It will be instructive to start out using our previous results above to handle the case of abelian connections, many of whose lessons with the divergences can be transferred onto the nonabelian case.

Consider a singular abelian connection
A_{z}=\sum_{j}\frac{b_{j}}{z-p_{j}}
,
A_{\overline{z}}=0
with each
b_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
\sum_{j}b_{j}=0
. This connection has monodromy
e^{2\pi ib_{j}}
around the puncture
p_{j}
. This will give us a field strength of
F=2\pi i\sum_{j}b_{j}\delta^{2}(x-x_{j})
. Proceeding naively, this will give an expression

\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}=-\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}(2\pi ib_{i})(2\pi ib_{j})\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})=+\sum_{i<j}b_{i}b_{j}\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})+\sum_{j}\frac{1}{2}b_{j}^{2}\log(|0|^{2}).

(151)

The infiniteness of the
\log(|0|^{2})
terms in this expression can be traced back to the fact that our gauge fields were singular. However, note that if we treat this infinity as a constant, the tau function agrees exactly with the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations Eq. (
130
).

To actually get a finite expression, one thing we could do is to ‘smooth out’ the connection by convolving it with a Gaussian
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\pi\epsilon}e^{-|x-x^{\prime}|^{2}/\epsilon}
and considering the expansion as
\epsilon\to 0
. In this limit, the terms
\sum_{i<j}b_{i}b_{j}\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})
will be preserved up to lower-order terms in
\epsilon
. However, the
\log(|0|^{2})
turns into

\begin{split}\log(|0|^{2})\to\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2})\frac{e^{-\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}{\epsilon}}}{(\pi\epsilon)^{2}}=\left(\int d^{2}\tilde{x}_{1}\log(|\tilde{x}_{1}|^{2})\frac{e^{-\frac{\tilde{x}_{1}^{2}}{2\epsilon}}}{\pi\epsilon}\right)\left(\int d^{2}\tilde{x}_{2}\frac{e^{-2\frac{\tilde{x}_{2}^{2}}{\epsilon}}}{\pi\epsilon}\right)=\log(2\epsilon)-\gamma_{\mathrm{Euler}}\end{split}

(152)

where
\tilde{x}_{1}=x_{1}-x_{2}
,
\tilde{x}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{2})
. This still a constant with respect to the
\{p_{1}\cdots p_{k}\}
that diverges logarithmically with
\epsilon
. This gives a smeared determinant

\left[\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\right]_{\textbf{smeared},\epsilon}=\sum_{i<j}b_{i}b_{j}\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})+\sum_{j}\frac{1}{2}b_{j}^{2}\left(\log(2\epsilon)-\gamma_{\mathrm{Euler}}\right)+O(\epsilon)

(153)

for abelian gauge fields. As such,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left\{\left[\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\right]_{\textbf{smeared},\epsilon}-\sum_{j}\frac{1}{2}b_{j}^{2}\log(\epsilon)\right\}

is well-defined and can be viewed as the tau function agreeing with the Eqs. (
130
) for abelian fields. In the next section, we argue that the series expansion for nonabelian gauge fields corresponds to the tau function.

Let’s generalize the calculation above to non-abelian Fuschian systems.
Here,
A_{\overline{z}}=0
and
A_{z}=\sum_{j}\frac{B_{j}}{z-p_{j}}
for matrices
\{B_{j}\}
satisfying
\sum_{j}B_{j}=0
, where the
\{B_{j}\}
depend on the
\{p_{j}\}
via the Schlesinger Equations (
128
). In this case, our series expansion becomes

\begin{split}\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}&-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{1}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}=:\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}\end{split}

(154)

where
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is the
n
th term in the expansion. To compare to the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations Eq. (
130
), we take the derivative with respect the
p_{j}
. First, we look at the
n=2
term. This calculation ends up working the same as the abelian case. In particular after a Gaussian smoothing by
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}
, we get

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\sum_{i<j}\mathrm{tr}\left[B_{i}B_{j}\right]\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})+\sum_{j}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[B_{j}^{2}\right]\left(\log(2\epsilon)-\gamma_{\mathrm{Euler}}\right)+O(\epsilon)

(155)

so that as
\epsilon\to 0

\begin{split}\partial_{p_{k}}\mathcal{I}_{2}&=\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{k}B_{j})}{p_{k}-p_{j}}+\sum_{i\neq j}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{[B_{k},B_{i}]}{p_{k}-p_{i}}-\delta_{k,i}\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\right)B_{j}\right]\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})\\
&=\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{k}B_{j})}{p_{k}-p_{j}}-\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{k}}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{1})}=:\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{k}B_{j})}{p_{k}-p_{j}}+\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{2}.\end{split}

(156)

In the first equality, the first term comes from the derivative of the ‘
\log(|p_{i}-p_{j}|^{2})
’ terms and the second comes from the Schlesinger equations and the variation of
\{B_{j}\}
, noting that
\mathrm{tr}\left[B_{j}^{2}\right]
remains invariant. The second equality just rewrites the second term, which we define as
\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{2}
in parallel with future notation. (See also the following derivation for an explanation for this rewriting of
\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{2}
.)

Note that the first term in the equality is what we want for the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations. We will see that
\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{2}
gets cancelled out by higher terms. Now, we write the derivative of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
for
n>2
:

\begin{split}\partial_{p_{k}}\mathcal{I}_{n}&=-\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{B_{k}}{(z_{1}-p_{k})^{2}}+\sum_{i}\left(\frac{[B_{k},B_{i}]}{p_{k}-p_{i}}-\delta_{k,i}\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\right)\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{i}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(-\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{B_{k}}{z_{1}-p_{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\\
&\quad\quad-\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{i}\frac{[B_{k},B_{i}]}{p_{k}-p_{i}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{i}}-\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi^{n-1}}\int d^{2}x_{2}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\,\,\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{B_{k}}{z_{2}-p_{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n-1}-\overline{z}_{n})(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{2})}-\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{B_{k}}{z_{n}-p_{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n-1}-\overline{z}_{n})}\right\}\\
&\quad\quad-\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{k}}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\\
&=+\frac{1}{\pi^{n-1}}\int d^{2}x_{2}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{z_{2}-p_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{z_{2}-p_{k}}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{3}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n-1}-\overline{z}_{n})(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{2})}\\
&\quad\quad-\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\ell}\frac{[B_{k},B_{\ell}]}{p_{k}-p_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{k}}\right)\right)\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{2}-p_{i}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i}\frac{B_{i}}{z_{n}-p_{i}}\right)\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\\
&=:\delta_{k}^{(1)}\mathcal{I}_{n}+\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{n},\end{split}

(157)

where
\delta_{k}^{(1)}\mathcal{I}_{n}
and
\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{n}
are the first and second terms of the second-to-last line above. In the first equality, the cyclicity of the trace gets rid of the
1/n
factor and lets us put the derivative completely in the first
A_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})
factor. In the second equality, the two terms from the derivatives of the
\left\{\frac{1}{z_{1}-p_{i}}\right\}
factors and of the
\{B_{j}\}
factors respectively are split up, and the first term is rewritten as a derivative with respect
z_{1}
. The third equality is a few steps. First, the second term is rewritten whose form matches for
n=2
as well as
n>2
. Then for the first term, we integrate the
\partial_{z_{1}}
by parts giving
\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{1}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}=\pi\left(\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x_{2})-\delta^{2}(x_{1}-x_{n})\right)\frac{1}{(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{3})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{2})}
, which requires
n>2
. Then, integration over
x_{1}
gives what is written. In the fourth line, the first term is rewritten by changing variables for one half of the integrand, expanding terms in partial fractions, and recollecting terms.

Note that for these terms, we have
\delta_{k}^{(2)}\mathcal{I}_{n}+\delta_{k}^{(1)}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}=0
for all
n\geq 2
. As such, the derivative of the series expansion will have all terms telescope away except for
\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\mathrm{tr}(B_{k}B_{j})}{p_{k}-p_{j}}
. This is what we wanted to show.

Above, we didn’t deal with the smearing procedure for the terms with
n>2
. In general, we conjecture that the only divergence comes from the
n=2
term and that after subtracting away the divergence
\sum_{j}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[B_{j}^{2}\right]\log(\epsilon)
, the series expansion above (potentially after some resummation procedure) has a well defined limit as
\epsilon\to 0
.

D.4
‘Zipper’ expansion of
\log\tau

Now, let us use zipper representations of flat gauge fields to expand the Dirac determinant. In this section, we consider a gauge field localized on
s
non-intersecting path segments
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{s}
which may share endpoints and that’s flat away from these endpoints. Then, after orienting the path segments, we assign a matrix of
U_{j}=e^{u_{j}}\in GL(N,\mathbb{C})
that gives the path-ordered exponential going right-to-left across
\gamma_{j}
. See Fig.
24
for an example.

Figure 24:
Example zipper representation of flat gauge field.

This gauge field corresponds to a connection
d-A
for a
\mathfrak{gl}(N,\mathbb{C})
-valued 1-form
A
which we write as

A=\sum_{j=1}^{s}A^{(j)}\quad\text{where}\quad A^{(j)}:=u_{j}\,\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j}).

(158)

Here, we define the singular 1-form-valued distribution
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})
whose fundamental property is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})\wedge v:=\int_{\gamma_{j}}v

(159)

for 1-forms
v
. Soon, we’ll give a concrete construction of this form.

Say that
\gamma_{j}
goes between
q_{j}\to q^{\prime}_{j}
. Then, we claim that the differential of this form acts as a difference of delta functions, that
(d\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j}))(x)=(\delta^{2}(x-q^{\prime}_{j})-\delta^{2}(x-q_{j}))d^{2}x
, where
d^{2}x=dx\wedge dy
is the volume form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. To see this, let
g\in C^{1}(\mathbb{C})
be a smooth test function. Then,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g\,d\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d(g\,\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j}))+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})\wedge dg=\int_{\gamma_{j}}dg=g(q^{\prime}_{j})-g(q_{j})=\int g(x)(\delta^{2}(x-q^{\prime}_{j})-\delta^{2}(x-q_{j}))d^{2}x.

(160)

The first equality uses a differential forms identity. The second one uses that
g\,\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})
vanishes at infinity and the definition of
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})
. So, the monodromy of the restricted connection
d-u_{j}\,\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})
going counterclockwise around
q_{j}
or clockwise around
p^{\prime}_{j}
is
e^{u_{j}}
. Since the connection is zero away from
\gamma_{j}
, the path-ordered exponential across
\gamma_{j}
is also
e^{u_{j}}
.

Now, let’s describe the distribution
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})
as the
\epsilon\to 0
limit of a collection of ‘regulated’ 1-forms
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})^{\epsilon}
. Let
\gamma_{j}^{L,\epsilon}
,
\gamma_{j}^{R,\epsilon}
be two curves between
p_{j}\to p^{\prime}_{j}
, such that
\gamma_{j}^{L,\epsilon}

[resp.
\gamma_{j}^{R,\epsilon}
]
is on the left
[resp. right]
side of
\gamma_{j}
, and that they’re within a distance
\epsilon
of
\gamma_{j}
. Then,
\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\epsilon}
is a region bounded by
\overline{\gamma_{j}^{L,\epsilon}}\bigsqcup\gamma_{j}^{R,\epsilon}
containing
\gamma_{j}
. Now, let
f^{\epsilon}
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}-\{p_{j},p^{\prime}_{j}\}
that takes
0<f(x)<1
for all
x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\epsilon})
and so that
f(x)=0

[resp.
f(x)=1
]
on
\gamma_{j}^{L,\epsilon}

[resp.
\gamma_{j}^{R,\epsilon}
]
. Then, we define our regulated 1-forms as

(\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})^{\epsilon})(x)=\begin{cases}(df^{\epsilon})(x)&\text{ if }x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{R}_{j}^{\epsilon})\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

(161)

See Fig.
25
for a depiction.

Figure 25:
A depiction of
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})^{\epsilon}
(purple vector field) which is the gradient of the function
f^{\epsilon}
(the white-red color gradient) as well as the auxiliary objects in the construction. The curves
\gamma_{j}^{L,\epsilon}
,
\gamma_{j}^{R,\epsilon}
are within a distance
\epsilon
of
\gamma_{j}
.

Now, we argue that the
\epsilon\to 0
limit of these forms satisfies the defining equation Eq. (
159
). Let
v
be a sufficiently regular 1-form. Then,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})^{\epsilon}\wedge v=\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}}df^{\epsilon}\wedge v=-\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}}f^{\epsilon}dv+\int_{\partial\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}}f\,v\xrightarrow{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\gamma_{j}}v.

(162)

The first equality uses that
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{j})^{\epsilon}=0
outside of
\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}
. The second equality uses a differential forms identity and Stokes’ theorem. In last equality, first we use
\int_{\partial\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}}f\,v=\int_{\gamma^{R,\epsilon}_{j}}v
which tends to
\int_{\gamma_{j}}v
. Then, with mild regularity conditions on
v
, the term
\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\epsilon}_{j}}f^{\epsilon}dv
vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
. For our cases of interest, we will choose
v
to be closed which would give an equality to
\int_{\gamma^{R,\epsilon}_{j}}v
even for finite
\epsilon
.

To compare to the zipper representation as in Fig.
23
, note that some of the endpoints
\{q_{j},q^{\prime}_{j}\}
will correspond to punctures and an associated monodromy, while others won’t be punctures and will just be places where the zippers meet. A subtle point is that there may be a puncture at a point
p
even though the monodromy around
p
is trivial. This will happen when the loop going around
p
traces out a nontrivial loop in
\pi_{1}(GL(N,\mathbb{C}))=\mathbb{Z}
. For example, this happens with the abelian connection
A^{\prime}_{\overline{z}}=0
,
A^{\prime}_{z}=\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z-1}
for which the monodromies around
p=0,1
are trivial but trace out nontrivial paths in
\mathbb{C}-\{0\}
.

Now we want to expand the Dirac determinant Eq. (
147
) with the correction Eq. (
158
). First, recall that we can expand the determinant as

\begin{split}&\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}\log\left(\text{1}-\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\sum_{j=1}^{s}\not{A^{(j)}}\right)+\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\left(\sum_{j=1}^{s}A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{s}A^{(j)}_{z}\right)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j)}_{z}(x_{1})A^{(j)}_{z}(x_{2})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}x\,A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x)A^{(j)}_{z}(x)\right)\\
&\quad-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\in\{1\cdots s\}\\
!(j_{1}=\cdots=j_{n})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{n}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j_{1})}_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})\cdots A^{(j_{n})}_{\overline{z}}(x_{n})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j_{1})}_{z}(x_{1})\cdots A^{(j_{n}}_{z}(x_{n})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{n}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}\end{split}

(163)

In the first equality we just split up
A
into its zipper components. In the second equality, we use a few facts.
First,
A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x)A^{(j)}_{z}(x)=0
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
since the zippers are disjoint. Then from the same reasoning as the abelian connections, the terms in the expansion
-\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j)}}\right)^{n}\right]
for
n>2
disappear. The other terms are the expressions for
-\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{1})}}\cdots\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{n})}}\right]
for
j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}
not all equal.

First, using Eq. (
149
) and the fact that
dA^{(j)}=u_{j}(\delta^{2}(x-q_{j})-\delta^{2}(x-q^{\prime}_{j}))d^{2}x

\begin{split}&-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\int d^{2}x_{2}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{2})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(j)}_{z}(x_{1})A^{(j)}_{z}(x_{2})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int d^{2}xA^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}A^{(j)}_{z}\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}\left[u_{j}^{2}\right]\left(\log(|q^{\prime}_{j}-q_{j}|^{2})-\log(|0|^{2})\right)\end{split}

(164)

where
\log(|0|^{2})
is an infinite constant as in Eq. (
152
).

Now, we will expand and re-sum the rest of the terms of Eq. (
163
). First, we will consider the case of the partial sum over the terms of the form

\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]

for
k\geq 1
which will add up to a sum

\begin{split}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{k+1}}\int d^{2}x_{1}\cdots\int d^{2}x_{k+1}\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(1)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{1})\cdots A^{(1)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{k})A^{(2)}_{\overline{z}}(x_{k+1})\right]}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{k+1}-z_{1})}+\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[A^{(1)}_{z}(x_{1})\cdots A^{(1)}_{z}(x_{k})A^{(2)}_{z}(x_{k+1})\right]}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})\cdots(\overline{z}_{k+1}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}.\end{split}

Note we don’t include the pre-factor
\frac{1}{k+1}
because the cyclicity of the trace will give
k+1
equal terms corresponding to
k
above.

The main point in analyzing the above integrals are the observations that

A^{(j)}_{\overline{z}}d^{2}x=\frac{1}{2i}A^{(j)}\wedge dz\quad,\quad A^{(j)}_{z}d^{2}x=\frac{1}{-2i}A^{(j)}\wedge d\overline{z}.

(165)

This observation combined with the defining property Eq. (
159
) means that we can replace the integrals over
d^{2}x
with holomorphic and antiholomorphic integrals over the curves
\{\gamma_{j}\}
.

For example, the
k=1
term above looks like

\begin{split}&\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]=-\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}u_{2}]}{\pi^{2}}\left\{\int_{\gamma_{1}}\frac{dz_{1}}{2i}\int_{\gamma_{2}}\frac{dz_{2}}{2i}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{1})}+\int_{\gamma_{1}}\frac{d\overline{z}_{1}}{-2i}\int_{\gamma_{2}}\frac{d\overline{z}_{2}}{-2i}\frac{1}{(\overline{z}_{1}-\overline{z}_{2})(\overline{z}_{2}-\overline{z}_{1})}\right\}\\
&=+\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}u_{2}]}{(2\pi)^{2}}\log\left|\frac{(q^{\prime}_{2}-q^{\prime}_{1})(q_{2}-q_{1})}{(q^{\prime}_{2}-q_{1})(q_{2}-q^{\prime}_{1})}\right|^{2}.\end{split}

(166)

This is not singular if
\gamma_{1}:q_{1}\to q^{\prime}_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}:q_{2}\to q^{\prime}_{2}
don’t share any endpoints, but has a logarithmic divergence otherwise.

Applying this prescription naively with other terms
k>1
leads to the expression

-\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]=\text{``}-\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}^{k}u_{2}]\left\{\frac{1}{(-2\pi i)^{k+1}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{1}}dz_{k}\int_{\gamma_{2}}dz_{k+1}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{k+1}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}\right\}\text{''}

where
\mathrm{c.c.}
refers to complex conjugation. This expression is singular for along the given domains. However, by the discussion around Eqs. (
161
,
162
) about the smeared out
\delta^{\perp}(\gamma_{1})^{\epsilon}
, together with the holomorphicity of the integrands
\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{k+1}-z_{1})}
implies that the contour
\gamma_{j}
gets split up in the following way. Let
\gamma_{j}^{(1)},\cdots,\gamma_{j}^{(k)}
be non-intersecting curves
q_{j}\to q^{\prime}_{j}
close to
\gamma_{j}
so that
\gamma_{j}^{(k)}\to\cdots\to\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
is the left-to-right order relative to the orientation of
\gamma_{j}
(see Fig.
26
). Then, the correct expression is

-\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]=-\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}^{k}u_{2}]}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(k)}\left\{\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{k+1}}\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(\sigma(1))}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(\sigma(k))}}dz_{k}\int_{\gamma_{2}}dz_{k+1}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{k+1}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}\right\}

(167)

so that we split up the contours for each variable
z_{1}\cdots z_{k}
into disjoint non-intersecting contours in all possible ways with equal weight.

Figure 26:
Splitting the contour
\gamma_{j}
into a series of disjoint curves
\gamma_{j}^{(1)},\cdots,\gamma_{j}^{(k)}
with the left-to-right order being
\gamma_{j}^{(k)}\to\cdots\to\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
.

Now for a permutation
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
for any
\ell\geq 1
, we’ll define

\overline{[\sigma]}=\overline{[\sigma(1)\cdots\sigma(\ell)]}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\ell+1}}\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(\sigma(1))}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(\sigma(\ell))}}dz_{\ell}\int_{\gamma_{2}}dz_{\ell+1}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{\ell+1}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}

(168)

These quantities have many relations between them. For example, by the residue theorem and some convenient factors of
\frac{1}{2\pi i}
, we have
\overline{[21]}=\overline{[12]}-\overline{[1]}
. So, we can change the orders of the contours, sometimes at the cost of a residue which is a lower-order integral. In general, we have the relation

\overline{[\sigma(1)\cdots\sigma(m),\sigma(m+1)\cdots\sigma(\ell)]}=\begin{cases}\overline{[\sigma(1)\cdots\sigma(m+1),\sigma(m)\cdots\sigma(\ell)]}\mp\overline{[\sigma^{\prime}(1)\cdots\sigma^{\prime}(m)\cdots\sigma^{\prime}(\ell-1)]}\quad&\text{if }\sigma(m)=\sigma(m+1)\pm 1\\
\overline{[\sigma(1)\cdots\sigma(m+1),\sigma(m)\cdots\sigma(\ell)]}\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(169)

Above,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell-1)
is the permutation induced from
\sigma:\{1\cdots\ell\}\backslash\{m\}\to\{1\cdots\ell\}\backslash\{\sigma(m)\}
by preserving the ordering. Define (c.f.
Stanley and Rota 2000
)

\mathrm{desc}(\sigma):=\#\{m\,|\,\sigma(m)>\sigma(m+1)\}\quad,\quad A(\ell,m)=\#\{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)\,|\,\mathrm{desc}(\sigma)=m\}

(170)

as the number of
descents
of a permutation and the number of permutations with a fixed number of descents. These
A(k,m)
are called
Eulerian numbers
. Note that
A(k,m)=0
for
m>k-1
and define
A(0,m):=0

12
12

12

WARNING: This choice doesn’t agree with the standard convention that
A(0,m)=\delta_{m,0}
.
. They satisfy the generating relation

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{k}}{k!}A(k,m)\xi^{m}=\frac{\xi-1}{\xi-e^{(\xi-1)\eta}}-1.

(171)

For
\sigma\in\mathrm{Sym}(\ell)
, repeated applications of Eq. (
169
) gives

\overline{[\sigma]}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}(-1)^{i}{\mathrm{desc}(\sigma)\choose i}\overline{[1,2,\cdots,\ell-i]}

(172)

so that our full sum becomes

-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}^{k}u_{2}]}{k!}\sum_{m=0}^{k}A(k,m)\sum_{i=0}^{m}(-1)^{i}{m\choose i}\overline{[1,2,\cdots,k-i]}

(173)

Now we want to ask what the coefficient of
\overline{[1,2,\cdots,k-i]}
is in the sum above. To do this, we use generating functions.
For the function
C(t)
formed by replaced
\overline{[1,2,\cdots,\ell]}\mapsto t^{\ell}
in the sum above, the coefficient of
t^{\ell}
will give the coefficient of
\overline{[1,2,\cdots,\ell]}
. We have

\begin{split}C(t):=&-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}^{k}u_{2}]}{k!}A(k,m)\sum_{i=0}^{m}(-1)^{i}{m\choose i}t^{k-i}=-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}[u_{1}^{k}u_{2}]}{k!}A(k,m)t^{k}\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\right)^{m}\\
=&-\mathrm{tr}\left[\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(u_{1}t)^{k}}{k!}A(k,m)\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\right)^{m}\right\}u_{2}\right]=-\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{1-t(1-e^{-u_{1}})}-1\right)u_{2}\right]=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}t^{\ell}\mathrm{tr}\left[(1-e^{-u_{1}})^{\ell}u_{2}\right].\end{split}

(174)

In the second-to-last equality, we simplify after using the generating relation Eq. (
171
). This implies

-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(1)}}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(2)}}\right]=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[(1-e^{-u_{1}})^{\ell}u_{2}\right]\left\{\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\ell+1}}\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(1)}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{1}^{(\ell)}}dz_{\ell}\int_{\gamma_{2}}dz_{\ell+1}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{\ell+1}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}\right\}

(175)

In general, the same exact steps above can re-sum many groups of terms in the full series
\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}
. In particular, for some
n>1
, let
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
,
j_{2}\neq j_{3}
,
\cdots
,
j_{n}\neq j_{1}
, with each
j_{\cdots}\in\{1,\cdots,s\}
. Then, we have the equality

\begin{split}&\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{1})}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdots\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{n})}}\right)^{k_{n}}\right]=\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{\ell_{n}=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[(1-e^{-u_{j_{1}}})^{\ell_{1}}\cdots(1-e^{-u_{j_{n}}})^{\ell_{n}}\right]\\
&\times\Bigg\{\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}}\int_{\gamma_{j_{1}}^{(1)}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{j_{1}}^{(\ell_{1})}}dz_{\ell}\cdots\cdots\int_{\gamma_{j_{n}}^{(1)}}dz_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n-1}+1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{j_{n}}^{(\ell_{n})}}dz_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}\Bigg\}.\end{split}

(176)

Note that now, the series expansion is in terms of the
\{e^{-u_{j}}=U_{j}^{-1}\}
.

Now, we can rewrite the full partition function using some combinatorics. First, we have

\sum_{n^{\prime}=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime}_{1}\cdots j^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\in\{1\cdots s\}\\
!(j^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=j^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\end{subarray}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j^{\prime}_{1})}}\cdots\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})}}\right]=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\in\{1\cdots s\}\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2},\cdots,j_{n}\neq j_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{1})}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdots\left(\frac{1}{\not{\partial}}\not{A^{(j_{n})}}\right)^{k_{n}}\right].

(177)

This follows from cyclicity of the trace and noting that the total weight of an equivalence class of the terms modulo cyclic symmetry is the same on both sides.
Then we will combine Eqs. (
163
,
164
,
177
,
176
), which gives us (after an additional step of combinatorics which is the same as the reverse of the Eq. (
177
))

\begin{split}&\log\frac{\det\left(\not{\partial}-\not{A}\right)}{\det\left(\not{\partial}\right)}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textbf{corr}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}\left[u_{j}^{2}\right]\log\left(\frac{|q^{\prime}_{j}-q_{j}|^{2}}{|0|^{2}}\right)\\
&\quad-\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\in\{1\cdots s\}\\
!(j_{1}=\cdots=j_{n})\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{tr}\left[(1-U_{j_{1}}^{-1})\cdots(1-U_{j_{n}}^{-1})\right]}\left\{\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\gamma_{j_{1}}^{(c(1))}}dz_{1}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{j_{n}}^{(c(n))}}dz_{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})\cdots(z_{n}-z_{1})}+\mathrm{c.c.}\right\}\end{split}

(178)

where the choice of
c(1)\cdots c(n)
satisfy
c(k)=1,c(k+1)=2,\cdots,c_{k+\ell}=\ell+1
for
j_{k-1}\neq j_{k}=\cdots=j_{k+\ell}\neq j_{k+\ell+1}
, the indices
k+n\sim k
being defined cyclically. This is just a cyclic reordering of the variables in the integration variables of Eq. (
176
).

Note that this series is entirely in terms of the monodromy representation
\{U_{j}^{-1}\}
.
However as we noted before, the
\tau
function depends not only on the monodromy data but changes under an action of
\pi_{1}(GL(N,\mathbb{C}))=\mathbb{Z}
acting on a puncture that twists the flat connection. (See for example the dicussion before Eq. (
147
) about large gauge transformations and the result for the abelian case Eq. (
149
).)
As such, the series above should not be expected to converge to the desired result (even after subtracting off appropriate logarithmic divergences) for all choices of
\{U_{j}\}
, but should be viewed as a series whose analytic continuation corresponds to the
\tau
function.

Also, note that the the holomorphic and antiholomorphic integrals above evaluate to holomorphic and antiholomorphic functions in the punctures. After splitting up the logarithms into their holmorphic and antiholomorphic components, we find that the entire regularized determinant splits up into a holomorphic and antiholomorphic piece.

The fact that the Fuschian representation of Sec.
D.3
satisfies the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno equations leads us to identify the holomorphic piece with the original tau function of
Jimbo et al. 1981
.
The antiholomorphic piece should be the conjugate of the tau function corresponding to the conjugate monodromies
\overline{U}_{1},\cdots,\overline{U}_{s}
.

On this note, we conjecture that the regulation of divergent integrals as appeared in Sec.
IV.4
for the dimer model asymptotically gives the Jimbo-Miwa-Ueno tau function.
